Continuing work from thread T-019b22cc-7eae-750e-92dc-a62e57a18d8f. When you lack specific information you can use read_thread to get it.

@src/syntax/parser.rs @src/syntax/highlights.rs @src/syntax/languages.rs @src/update/syntax.rs @src/update/document.rs @src/model/document.rs @src/runtime/app.rs @queries/javascript/highlights.scm @queries/html/highlights.scm @queries/css/highlights.scm @queries/yaml/highlights.scm @queries/markdown/highlights.scm

I implemented syntax highlighting using tree-sitter with an async background parsing architecture:

- Background worker thread receives parse requests via mpsc channel
- 50ms debouncing prevents parsing on every keystroke
- Revision-based staleness checks discard outdated results
- Supports YAML, Markdown, Rust, HTML, CSS, JavaScript

Key bugs I fixed in this session:
- Worker loop was draining all requests globally, keeping only the last one - fixed by using per-document HashMap to keep latest request per document
- Tree-sitter returns byte columns but I was treating them as character indices - added byte_to_char_col() conversion
- Stale highlights showed during edits - now clearing syntax_highlights in push_edit()

Current issue: Syntax highlighting does not update when document is edited. The redraw_with_syntax_parse() helper should batch Cmd::Redraw with Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse for the current document, but something is broken.

The JavaScript highlight query file (queries/javascript/highlights.scm) has errors that cause it to fail at runtime - need to test that .scm files compile correctly.

Architecture:
- src/syntax/parser.rs - ParserState with parsers, trees, queries per language. init_language() creates parser and compiles Query from .scm file
- src/syntax/worker.rs - handles async parsing
- src/syntax/highlights.rs - HighlightToken, LineHighlights, SyntaxHighlights data structures
- src/syntax/languages.rs - LanguageId enum with from_extension(), has_highlighting()
- src/update/syntax.rs - handles SyntaxMsg::ParseReady and ParseCompleted, schedule_syntax_parse() helper
- src/update/document.rs - redraw_with_syntax_parse() helper that should trigger parsing after edits
- src/model/document.rs - Document stores syntax_highlights: Option<SyntaxHighlights>, push_edit() clears highlights
- src/runtime/app.rs - syntax_worker_loop() processes requests, trigger_initial_syntax_parsing() on startup

Query files are in queries/{yaml,markdown,html,css,javascript}/highlights.scm - they get included via include_str!() and compiled with Query::new()

Test coverage needed:
- Verify .scm files compile without errors (Query::new should succeed)
- Test that editing triggers re-parsing
- Test that highlights update after parse completes
- Test revision staleness checks work correctly

syntax highlighting on the initial run works fine now, we need to add a test/assertion that the scm files compile correctly (run make dev to see it error out on javascript files atm), it does however not update when changes are done to the document, we need to add comprehensive tests to iron out issues with  syntax highlighting.

{
  "diff": "```diff\nIndex: ~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs\n===================================================================\n--- ~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs\toriginal\n+++ ~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs\tmodified\n@@ -919,4 +919,40 @@\n             }\n         }\n     }\n }\n+\n+/// Syntax highlighting worker thread loop\n+fn syntax_worker_loop(rx: Receiver<SyntaxParseRequest>, msg_tx: Sender<Msg>) {\n+    use std::collections::HashMap;\n+\n+    let mut parser_state = ParserState::new();\n+    let mut pending: HashMap<token::model::editor_area::DocumentId, SyntaxParseRequest> =\n+        HashMap::new();\n+\n+    loop {\n+        // Wait for first request (blocking)\n+        let request = match rx.recv() {\n+            Ok(req) => req,\n+            Err(_) => return, // Channel closed\n+        };\n+        pending.insert(request.document_id, request);\n+\n+        // Drain any additional pending requests (non-blocking)\n+        // Keep only the latest request per document\n+        while let Ok(req) = rx.try_recv() {\n+            pending.insert(req.document_id, req);\n+        }\n+\n+        // Process all pending requests\n+        for (_doc_id, req) in pending.drain() {\n+            let highlights =\n+                parser_state.parse_and_highlight(&req.source, req.language, req.document_id, req.revision);\n+\n+            let _ = msg_tx.send(Msg::Syntax(SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted {\n+                document_id: req.document_id,\n+                revision: req.revision,\n+                highlights,\n+            }));\n+        }\n+    }\n+}\n```",

[

    "title": "Static Node Types - Tree-sitter",
    "excerpts": [
      "## Keyboard shortcuts\n\nPress `←` or `→` to navigate between chapters\n\nPress `S` or `/` to search in the book\n\nPress `?` to show this help\n\nPress `Esc` to hide this help\n\n* Auto\n* Light\n* Rust\n* Coal\n* Navy\n* Ayu\n\n# Tree-sitter\n\n[](../print.html \"Print this book\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter \"Git repository\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/edit/master/docs/src/using-parsers/6-static-node-types.md \"Suggest an edit\")\n\n# [Static Node Types]()\n\nIn languages with static typing, it can be helpful for syntax trees to provide specific type information about individual\nsyntax nodes. Tree-sitter makes this information available via a generated file called `node-types.json` . This _node types_ file provides structured data about every possible syntax node in a grammar.\n\nYou can use this data to generate type declarations in statically-typed programming languages.\n\nThe node types file contains an array of objects, each of which describes a particular type of syntax node using the\nfollowing entries:\n\n## [Basic Info]()\n\nEvery object in this array has these two entries:\n\n* `\"type\"` — A string that indicates, which grammar rule the node represents. This corresponds to the `ts_node_type` function\n  described [here](./2-basic-parsing.html) .\n* `\"named\"` — A boolean that indicates whether this kind of node corresponds to a rule name in the grammar or just a string\n  literal. See [here](./2-basic-parsing.html) for more info.\n\nExamples:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"string_literal\",\n  \"named\": true\n}\n{\n  \"type\": \"+\",\n  \"named\": false\n}\n```\n\nTogether, these two fields constitute a unique identifier for a node type; no two top-level objects in the `node-types.json` should have the same values for both `\"type\"` and `\"named\"` .\n\n## [Internal Nodes]()\n\nMany syntax nodes can have _children_ . The node type object describes the possible children that a node can have using the\nfollowing entries:\n\n* `\"fields\"` — An object that describes the possible [fields](./2-basic-parsing.html) that the node can have. The keys of this\n  object are field names, and the values are _child type_ objects, described below.\n* `\"children\"` — Another _child type_ object that describes all the node's possible _named_ children _without_ fields.\n\nA _child type_ object describes a set of child nodes using the following entries:\n\n* `\"required\"` — A boolean indicating whether there is always _at least one_ node in this set.\n* `\"multiple\"` — A boolean indicating whether there can be _multiple_ nodes in this set.\n* `\"types\"` \\- An array of objects that represent the possible types of nodes in this set. Each object has two keys: `\"type\"` and `\"named\"` , whose meanings are described above.\n\nExample with fields:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"method_definition\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"fields\": {\n    \"body\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": true,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"statement_block\", \"named\": true }]\n    },\n    \"decorator\": {\n      \"multiple\": true,\n      \"required\": false,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"decorator\", \"named\": true }]\n    },\n    \"name\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": true,\n      \"types\": [\n        { \"type\": \"computed_property_name\", \"named\": true },\n        { \"type\": \"property_identifier\", \"named\": true }\n      ]\n    },\n    \"parameters\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": true,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"formal_parameters\", \"named\": true }]\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\nExample with children:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"array\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"fields\": {},\n  \"children\": {\n    \"multiple\": true,\n    \"required\": false,\n    \"types\": [\n      { \"type\": \"_expression\", \"named\": true },\n      { \"type\": \"spread_element\", \"named\": true }\n    ]\n  }\n}\n```\n\n## [Supertype Nodes]()\n\nIn Tree-sitter grammars, there are usually certain rules that represent abstract _categories_ of syntax nodes (e.g. \"expression\",\n\"type\", \"declaration\"). In the `grammar.js` file, these are often written as [hidden rules](../creating-parsers/3-writing-the-grammar.html) whose definition is a simple [`choice`](../creating-parsers/2-the-grammar-dsl.html) where each member is just a single symbol.\n\nNormally, hidden rules are not mentioned in the node types file, since they don't appear in the syntax tree. But if you add\na hidden rule to the grammar's [`supertypes` list](../creating-parsers/2-the-grammar-dsl.html) , then it _will_ show up in the node\ntypes file, with the following special entry:\n\n* `\"subtypes\"` — An array of objects that specify the _types_ of nodes that this 'supertype' node can wrap.\n\nExample:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"_declaration\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"subtypes\": [\n    { \"type\": \"class_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"function_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"generator_function_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"lexical_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"variable_declaration\", \"named\": true }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nSupertype nodes will also appear elsewhere in the node types file, as children of other node types, in a way that corresponds\nwith how the supertype rule was used in the grammar. This can make the node types much shorter and easier to read, because\na single supertype will take the place of multiple subtypes.\n\nExample:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"export_statement\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"fields\": {\n    \"declaration\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": false,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"_declaration\", \"named\": true }]\n    },\n    \"source\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": false,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"string\", \"named\": true }]\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n[](../using-parsers/queries/4-api.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../using-parsers/7-abi-versions.html \"Next chapter\")\n\n[](../using-parsers/queries/4-api.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../using-parsers/7-abi-versions.html \"Next chapter\")\n"
    ]
  },
  {
    "url": "https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/using-parsers/queries/1-syntax.html",
    "title": "Query Syntax - Tree-sitter",
    "excerpts": [
      "## Keyboard shortcuts\n\nPress `←` or `→` to navigate between chapters\n\nPress `S` or `/` to search in the book\n\nPress `?` to show this help\n\nPress `Esc` to hide this help\n\n* Auto\n* Light\n* Rust\n* Coal\n* Navy\n* Ayu\n\n# Tree-sitter\n\n[](../../print.html \"Print this book\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter \"Git repository\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/edit/master/docs/src/using-parsers/queries/1-syntax.md \"Suggest an edit\")\n\n# [Query Syntax]()\n\nA _query_ consists of one or more _patterns_ , where each pattern is an [S-expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-expression) that matches a certain set of\nnodes in a syntax tree. The expression to match a given node consists of a pair of parentheses containing two things: the\nnode's type, and optionally, a series of other S-expressions that match the node's children. For example, this pattern would\nmatch any `binary_expression` node whose children are both `number_literal` nodes:\n\n```\n(binary_expression (number_literal) (number_literal))\n```\n\nChildren can also be omitted. For example, this would match any `binary_expression` where at least _one_ of child is a `string_literal` node:\n\n```\n(binary_expression (string_literal))\n```\n\n## [Fields]()\n\nIn general, it's a good idea to make patterns more specific by specifying [field names](../2-basic-parsing.html) associated with\nchild nodes. You do this by prefixing a child pattern with a field name followed by a colon. For example, this pattern would\nmatch an `assignment_expression` node where the `left` child is a `member_expression` whose `object` is a `call_expression` .\n\n```\n(assignment_expression\n  left: (member_expression\n    object: (call_expression)))\n```\n\n## [Negated Fields]()\n\nYou can also constrain a pattern so that it only matches nodes that _lack_ a certain field. To do this, add a field name\nprefixed by a `!` within the parent pattern. For example, this pattern would match a class declaration with no type parameters:\n\n```\n(class_declaration\n  name: (identifier) @class_name\n  !type_parameters)\n```\n\n## [Anonymous Nodes]()\n\nThe parenthesized syntax for writing nodes only applies to [named nodes](../2-basic-parsing.html) . To match specific anonymous\nnodes, you write their name between double quotes. For example, this pattern would match any `binary_expression` where the\noperator is `!=` and the right side is `null` :\n\n```\n(binary_expression\n  operator: \"!=\"\n  right: (null))\n```\n\n## [Special Nodes]()\n\n### [The Wildcard Node]()\n\nA wildcard node is represented with an underscore ( `_` ), it matches any node.\nThis is similar to `.` in regular expressions.\nThere are two types, `(_)` will match any named node,\nand `_` will match any named or anonymous node.\n\nFor example, this pattern would match any node inside a call:\n\n```\n(call (_) @call.inner)\n```\n\n### [The `ERROR` Node]()\n\nWhen the parser encounters text it does not recognize, it represents this node\nas `(ERROR)` in the syntax tree. These error nodes can be queried just like\nnormal nodes:\n\n```\n(ERROR) @error-node\n```\n\n### [The `MISSING` Node]()\n\nIf the parser is able to recover from erroneous text by inserting a missing token and then reducing, it will insert that\nmissing node in the final tree so long as that tree has the lowest error cost. These missing nodes appear as seemingly normal\nnodes in the tree, but they are zero tokens wide, and are internally represented as a property of the actual terminal node\nthat was inserted, instead of being its own kind of node, like the `ERROR` node. These special missing nodes can be queried\nusing `(MISSING)` :\n\n```\n(MISSING) @missing-node\n```\n\nThis is useful when attempting to detect all syntax errors in a given parse tree, since these missing node are not captured\nby `(ERROR)` queries. Specific missing node types can also be queried:\n\n```\n(MISSING identifier) @missing-identifier\n(MISSING \";\") @missing-semicolon\n```\n\n### [Supertype Nodes]()\n\nSome node types are marked as _supertypes_ in a grammar. A supertype is a node type that contains multiple\nsubtypes. For example, in the [JavaScript grammar example](../../creating-parsers/3-writing-the-grammar.html) , `expression` is a supertype that can represent any kind\nof expression, such as a `binary_expression` , `call_expression` , or `identifier` . You can use supertypes in queries to match\nany of their subtypes, rather than having to list out each subtype individually. For example, this pattern would match any\nkind of expression, even though it's not a visible node in the syntax tree:\n\n```\n(expression) @any-expression\n```\n\nTo query specific subtypes of a supertype, you can use the syntax `supertype/subtype` . For example, this pattern would\nmatch a `binary_expression` only if it is a child of `expression` :\n\n```\n(expression/binary_expression) @binary-expression\n```\n\nThis also applies to anonymous nodes. For example, this pattern would match `\"()\"` only if it is a child of `expression` :\n\n```\n(expression/\"()\") @empty-expression\n```\n\n[](../../using-parsers/queries/index.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../../using-parsers/queries/2-operators.html \"Next chapter\")\n\n[](../../using-parsers/queries/index.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../../using-parsers/queries/2-operators.html \"Next chapter\")\n"
    ]
  },

all\nfiles |\n\n## Repository files navigation\n\n* [README](#)\n* [MIT license](#)\n\n# Node Tree-sitter\n\n[]()\n\n[](https://github.com/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/actions/workflows/ci.yml) [](https://npmjs.com/package/tree-sitter) [](https://tree-sitter.github.io/node-tree-sitter)\n\nThis module provides Node.js bindings to the [tree-sitter] parsing library. ## Installation\n\n[]()\n\n```\nnpm install tree-sitter\n```\n\n## Basic Usage\n\n[]()\n\n### Prerequisites\n\n[]()\n\nFirst, you'll need a Tree-sitter grammar for the language you want to parse. There are many [existing grammars](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/wiki/List-of-parsers) ,\nsuch as [tree-sitter-javascript](http://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter-javascript) . These grammars can typically be installed with a package manager like NPM,\nso long as the author has published them.\n```\nnpm install tree-sitter-javascript\n```\n\nYou can also develop a new grammar by using the [Tree-sitter CLI](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/tree/master/cli) and following the [docs](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/creating-parsers) . ### Parsing Source Code\n\n[]()\n\nOnce you've got your grammar, create a parser with that grammar. ```\nconst Parser = require ( 'tree-sitter' ) ; \n const JavaScript = require ( 'tree-sitter-javascript' ) ; \n\n const parser = new Parser ( ) ; \n parser . setLanguage ( JavaScript ) ;\n```\n\nThen you can parse some source code,\n\n```\nconst sourceCode = 'let x = 1; console.log(x);' ; \n const tree = parser . parse ( sourceCode ) ;\n```\n\nand inspect the syntax tree. ```\nconsole . log ( tree . rootNode .\ntoString ( ) ) ; \n\n // (program \n //   (lexical_declaration \n //     (variable_declarator (identifier) (number))) \n //   (expression_statement \n //     (call_expression \n //       (member_expression (identifier) (property_identifier)) \n //       (arguments (identifier))))) \n\n const callExpression = tree . rootNode . child ( 1 ) . firstChild ; \n console . log ( callExpression ) ; \n\n // { \n //   type: 'call_expression', \n //   startPosition: {row: 0, column: 16}, \n //   endPosition: {row: 0, column: 30}, \n //   startIndex: 0, \n //   endIndex: 30 \n // }\n```\n\nIf your source code _changes_ , you can update the syntax tree. This is much faster than the first parse. ```\n// In the code, we replaced 'let' with 'const'. // So, we set our old end index to 3, and our new end index to 5. // Note that the end index is exclusive. const newSourceCode = 'const x = 1; console.log(x);' ; \n //                        ^ ^ \n // indices:               3 5 \n // points:            (0,3) (0,5) \n\n tree .\n ... \nOnce you're comfortable with the basics, you can explore the [full API documentation](https://tree-sitter.github.io/node-tree-sitter) ,\nwhich should map closely to the C API, though there are some differences. ## About\n\nNode.js bindings for tree-sitter\n\n[www.npmjs.com/package/tree-sitter](https://www.npmjs.com/package/tree-sitter \"https://www.npmjs.com/package/tree-sitter\")\n\n### Topics\n\n[nodejs](/topics/nodejs \"Topic: nodejs\") [javascript](/topics/javascript \"Topic: javascript\") [tree-sitter](/topics/tree-sitter \"Topic: tree-sitter\") [binding](/topics/binding \"Topic: binding\")\n\n### Resources\n\n[Readme]()\n\n### License\n\n[MIT license]()\n\n### Uh oh! There was an error while loading. Please reload this page .\n[Activity](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/activity)\n\n[Custom properties](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/custom-properties)\n\n### Stars\n\n[**804** stars](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/stargazers)\n\n### Watchers\n\n[**14** watching](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/watchers)\n\n### Forks\n\n[**152** forks](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/forks)\n\n[Report repository](/contact/report-content?content_url=https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2Ftree-sitter%2Fnode-tree-sitter&report=tree-sitter+%28user%29)\n\n## [Releases 54](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/releases)\n\n[v0.22.4 Latest Dec 30, 2024](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/releases/tag/v0.22.4)\n\n[\\+ 53 releases](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/releases)\n\n## Sponsor this project\n\n* \n\n### Uh oh! There was an error while loading. Please reload this page .\n ... \n[\\+ 16 contributors](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/graphs/contributors)\n\n## Languages\n\n* [JavaScript 59\\.9%](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/search?l=javascript)\n* [C++ 39\\.3%](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/search?l=c%2B%2B)\n* [Python 0\\.8%](/tree-sitter/node-tree-sitter/search?l=python)\n\n## Footer\n\n[](https://github.com) © 2025 GitHub, Inc.\n\n### Footer navigation\n\n* [Terms](https://docs.github.com/site-policy/github-terms/github-terms-of-service)\n* [Privacy](https://docs.github.com/site-policy/privacy-policies/github-privacy-statement)\n* [Security](https://github.com/security)\n* [Status](https://www.githubstatus.com/)\n* [Community](https://github.community/)\n* [Docs](https://docs.github.com/)\n* [Contact](https://support.github.com?tags=dotcom-footer)\n* Manage cookies\n* Do not share my personal information\n\nYou can’t perform that action at this time."

    "url": "https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/creating-parsers/2-the-grammar-dsl.html",
    "title": "The Grammar DSL - Tree-sitter",
    "excerpts": [
      "## Keyboard shortcuts\n\nPress `←` or `→` to navigate between chapters\n\nPress `S` or `/` to search in the book\n\nPress `?` to show this help\n\nPress `Esc` to hide this help\n\n* Auto\n* Light\n* Rust\n* Coal\n* Navy\n* Ayu\n\n# Tree-sitter\n\n[](../print.html \"Print this book\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter \"Git repository\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/edit/master/docs/src/creating-parsers/2-the-grammar-dsl.md \"Suggest an edit\")\n\n# [The Grammar DSL]()\n\nThe following is a complete list of built-in functions you can use in your `grammar.js` to define rules. Use-cases for some\nof these functions will be explained in more detail in later sections. * **Symbols (the `$` object)** — Every grammar rule is written as a JavaScript function that takes a parameter conventionally\n  called `$` . The syntax `$.identifier` is how you refer to another grammar symbol within a rule.\nNames starting with `$.MISSING` or `$.UNEXPECTED` should be avoided as they have special meaning for the `tree-sitter test` command. * **String and Regex literals** — The terminal symbols in a grammar are described using JavaScript strings and regular\n  expressions. Of course during parsing, Tree-sitter does not actually use JavaScript's regex engine to evaluate these regexes;\n  it generates its own regex-matching logic based on the Rust regex syntax as part of each parser. Regex literals are just\n  used as a convenient way of writing regular expressions in your grammar. You can use Rust regular expressions in your grammar\n  DSL through the `RustRegex` class. Simply pass your regex pattern as a string:\n  \n  ```\n  new RustRegex('(?i)[a-z_][a-z0-9_]*') // matches a simple identifier\n  ```\n  \n  Unlike JavaScript's builtin `RegExp` class, which takes a pattern and flags as separate arguments, `RustRegex` only\n  accepts a single pattern string.\nWhile it doesn't support separate flags, you can use inline flags within the pattern itself. For more details about Rust's regex syntax and capabilities, check out the [Rust regex documentation](https://docs.rs/regex/1.1.8/regex/) . Note\n  \n  []()\n  \n  Only a subset of the Regex engine is actually supported. This is due to certain features like lookahead and lookaround\n  assertions not feasible to use in an LR(1) grammar, as well as certain flags being unnecessary for tree-sitter. However,\n  plenty of features are supported by default:\n  \n    + Character classes\n    + Character ranges\n    + Character sets\n    + Quantifiers\n    + Alternation\n    + Grouping\n    + Unicode character escapes\n    + Unicode property escapes\n* **Sequences : `seq(rule1, rule2, ...)`** — This function creates a rule that matches any number of other rules, one after\n  another.\nIt is analogous to simply writing multiple symbols next to each other in [EBNF notation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_Backus%E2%80%93Naur_form) . * **Alternatives : `choice(rule1, rule2, ...)`** — This function creates a rule that matches _one_ of a set of possible\n  rules. The order of the arguments does not matter. This is analogous to the `|` (pipe) operator in EBNF notation. * **Repetitions : `repeat(rule)`** — This function creates a rule that matches _zero-or-more_ occurrences of a given rule. It is analogous to the `{x}` (curly brace) syntax in EBNF notation. * **Repetitions : `repeat1(rule)`** — This function creates a rule that matches _one-or-more_ occurrences of a given rule. The previous `repeat` rule is implemented in `repeat1` but is included because it is very commonly used. * **Options : `optional(rule)`** — This function creates a rule that matches _zero or one_ occurrence of a given rule. It is analogous to the `[x]` (square bracket) syntax in EBNF notation.\n ... \nThe purpose of lexical precedence is to solve the issue where multiple tokens can match the same\n  set of characters, but one token should be preferred over the other. See [Lexical Precedence vs Parse Precedence](./3-writing-the-grammar.html) for a more detailed explanation. * **Left Associativity : `prec.left([number], rule)`** — This function marks the given rule as left-associative (and optionally\n  applies a numerical precedence). When an LR(1) conflict arises in which all the rules have the same numerical precedence,\n  Tree-sitter will consult the rules' associativity. If there is a left-associative rule, Tree-sitter will prefer matching\n  a rule that ends _earlier_ . This works similarly to [associativity directives](https://docs.oracle.com/cd/E19504-01/802-5880/6i9k05dh3/index.html) in Yacc grammars. * **Right Associativity : `prec.right([number], rule)`** — This function is like `prec.left` , but it instructs Tree-sitter\n  to prefer matching a rule that ends _later_ .\n* **Dynamic Precedence : `prec.dynamic(number, rule)`** — This function is similar to `prec` , but the given numerical precedence\n  is applied at _runtime_ instead of at parser generation time. This is only necessary when handling a conflict dynamically\n  using the `conflicts` field in the grammar, and when there is a genuine _ambiguity_ : multiple rules correctly match a given\n  piece of code. In that event, Tree-sitter compares the total dynamic precedence associated with each rule, and selects the\n  one with the highest total. This is similar to [dynamic precedence directives](https://www.gnu.org/software/bison/manual/html_node/Generalized-LR-Parsing.html) in Bison grammars. * **Tokens : `token(rule)`** — This function marks the given rule as producing only\n  a single token. Tree-sitter's default is to treat each String or RegExp literal\n  in the grammar as a separate token. Each token is matched separately by the lexer\n  and returned as its own leaf node in the tree.\nThe `token` function allows you to\n  express a complex rule using the functions described above (rather than as a single\n  regular expression) but still have Tree-sitter treat it as a single token. The token function will only accept terminal rules, so `token($.foo)` will not work. You can think of it as a shortcut for squashing complex rules of strings or regexes\n  down to a single token. * **Immediate Tokens : `token.immediate(rule)`** — Usually, whitespace (and any other extras, such as comments) is optional\n  before each token. This function means that the token will only match if there is no whitespace. * **Aliases : `alias(rule, name)`** — This function causes the given rule to _appear_ with an alternative name in the syntax\n  tree. If `name` is a _symbol_ , as in `alias($.foo, $.bar)` , then the aliased rule will _appear_ as a [named node](../using-parsers/2-basic-parsing.html) called `bar` .\nAnd if `name` is a _string literal_ , as in `alias($.foo, 'bar')` , then the aliased rule will appear as an [anonymous node](../using-parsers/2-basic-parsing.html) , as if the rule had been written as the simple string. * **Field Names : `field(name, rule)`** — This function assigns a _field name_ to the child node(s) matched by the given\n  rule. In the resulting syntax tree, you can then use that field name to access specific children. * **Reserved Keywords : `reserved(wordset, rule)`** — This function will override the global reserved word set with the\n  one passed into the `wordset` parameter. This is useful for contextual keywords, such as `if` in JavaScript, which cannot\n  be used as a variable name in most contexts, but can be used as a property name. In addition to the `name` and `rules` fields, grammars have a few other optional public fields that influence the behavior\nof the parser.\nEach of these fields is a function that accepts the grammar object ( `$` ) as its only parameter, like the\ngrammar rules themselves. These fields are:\n\n* **`extras`** — an array of tokens that may appear _anywhere_ in the language. This is often used for whitespace and\n  comments. The default value of `extras` is to accept whitespace. To control whitespace explicitly, specify `extras: $ => []` in your grammar. See the section on [using extras](./3-writing-the-grammar.html) for more details. * **`inline`** — an array of rule names that should be automatically _removed_ from the grammar by replacing all of their\n  usages with a copy of their definition. This is useful for rules that are used in multiple places but for which you _don't_ want to create syntax tree nodes at runtime. * **`conflicts`** — an array of arrays of rule names. Each inner array represents a set of rules that's involved in an _LR(1) conflict_ that is _intended to exist_ in the grammar.\nWhen these conflicts occur at runtime, Tree-sitter will use\n  the GLR algorithm to explore all the possible interpretations. If _multiple_ parses end up succeeding, Tree-sitter will pick\n  the subtree whose corresponding rule has the highest total _dynamic precedence_ . * **`externals`** — an array of token names which can be returned by an [_external scanner_](./4-external-scanners.html) . External scanners allow you to write custom C code which runs during the lexing\n  process to handle lexical rules (e.g. Python's indentation tokens) that cannot be described by regular expressions. * **`precedences`** — an array of arrays of strings, where each array of strings defines named precedence levels in descending\n  order. These names can be used in the `prec` functions to define precedence relative only to other names in the array, rather\n  than globally. Can only be used with parse precedence, not lexical precedence.\n* **`word`** — the name of a token that will match keywords to the [keyword extraction](./3-writing-the-grammar.html) optimization. * **`supertypes`** — an array of rule names which should be considered to be 'supertypes' in the generated [_node types_ file](../using-parsers/6-static-node-types.html) . Supertype rules are automatically hidden from the parse tree, regardless\n  of whether their names start with an underscore. The main use case for supertypes is to group together multiple different\n  kinds of nodes under a single abstract category, such as \"expression\" or \"declaration\". See the section on [`using supertypes`](./3-writing-the-grammar.html) for more details. * **`reserved`** — similar in structure to the main `rules` property, an object of reserved word sets associated with an\n  array of reserved rules. The reserved rule in the array must be a terminal token meaning it must be a string, regex, token,\n  or terminal rule.\nThe reserved rule must also exist and be used in the grammar, specifying arbitrary tokens will not work. The _first_ reserved word set in the object is the global word set, meaning it applies to every rule in every parse state. However, certain keywords are contextual, depending on the rule. For example, in JavaScript, keywords are typically not allowed\n  as ordinary variables, however, they _can_ be used as a property name. In this situation, the `reserved` function would be used,\n  and the word set to pass in would be the name of the word set that is declared in the `reserved` object that corresponds to an\n  empty array, signifying _no_ keywords are reserved. [](../creating-parsers/1-getting-started.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../creating-parsers/3-writing-the-grammar.html \"Next chapter\")\n\n[](../creating-parsers/1-getting-started.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../creating-parsers/3-writing-the-grammar.html \"Next chapter\")"
    ]

If it doesn't have a home elsewhere, it belongs here\n\n[](//music.forem.com)\n\n[](//music.forem.com)\n\n### [Music Forem](//music.forem.com)\n\nFollow\n\nFrom composing and gigging to gear, hot music takes, and everything in between.\n[](//vibe.forem.com)\n\n[](//vibe.forem.com)\n\n### [Vibe Coding Forem](//vibe.forem.com)\n\nFollow\n\nDiscussing AI software development, and showing off what we're building. [](//popcorn.forem.com)\n\n[](//popcorn.forem.com)\n\n### [Popcorn Movies and TV](//popcorn.forem.com)\n\nFollow\n\nMovie and TV enthusiasm, criticism and everything in-between. [](//dumb.dev.to)\n\n[](//dumb.dev.to)\n\n### [DUMB DEV Community](//dumb.dev.to)\n\nFollow\n\nMemes and software development shitposting\n\n[](//design.forem.com)\n\n[](//design.forem.com)\n\n### [Design Community](//design.forem.com)\n\nFollow\n\nWeb design, graphic design and everything in-between\n\n[](//zeroday.forem.com)\n\n[](//zeroday.forem.com)\n\n### [Security Forem](//zeroday.forem.com)\n\nFollow\n\nYour central hub for all things security.\n ... \nGuide to Tree-sitter\n\n[\\# python](/t/python) [\\# javascript](/t/javascript) [\\# beginners](/t/beginners) [\\# programming](/t/programming)\n\nNavigating through extensive or complex code can be challenging, but **Tree-sitter** is here to assist. ## []() Key Background Knowledge\n\n### []() Tree-sitter\n\n[Tree-sitter](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/) generates parsers based on a language and provides insights about the code as seen by the engine. It takes that messy code and weaves it into a clear, structured map, revealing the relationships between different parts. This map, called an Abstract Syntax Tree (AST), acts like a visual blueprint, making complex code easier to understand.\n ... \n)\n```\n\nIn constructing the Abstract Syntax Tree (AST), the function definition is designated as the root node, with \"greet\" and \"name\" forming its direct descendants or children, illustrating the hierarchical relationship between the function and its components. The print statement is then integrated into the AST as another child node of the function definition, with a further breakdown of its internal syntactic structure to represent the operation and its operands as shown in the following image:\n\n[](https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=800%2Cheight=%2Cfit=scale-down%2Cgravity=auto%2Cformat=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2Fprhihaoe7u2nxyfh70ni.png)\n\nAST formed above can be visualised as a tree as follows:\n\n[](https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=800%2Cheight=%2Cfit=scale-down%2Cgravity=auto%2Cformat=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2Frnrfpjmmevxxcy0nommp.png)\n\nIsn't this cool?\n### []() Using Tree-sitter in Your JavaScript Project\n\nWhile Tree-sitter itself is majorly writtern in Rust, there are JavaScript bindings that allow you to interact with it from your JavaScript code. Here's a glimpse into what you can do:\n\n1. **Installation:** First, you'll need to install the Tree-sitter library and the Python parser using npm:\n\n```\nnpm  install  tree-sitter tree-sitter-python  --legacy-peer-deps\n```\n\n> legacy-peer-deps flag is added due to peerDependencies conflict\n> \n> \n\n1. **Write Some Python Code:** Create a simple Python file with some code you'd like to analyze (like the `greet` function from before). Save this file as `your_code.py` . 2. **Import the Libraries:** In your JavaScript file, import the necessary libraries:\n\n```\nconst TreeSitter = require ( ' tree-sitter ' ); const Python = require ( ' tree-sitter-python ' ); const fs = require ( ' fs ' ). promises ;\n```\n\n1. **Parse the Code:** Use the libraries to parse your Python file and generate the AST.\nHere's how:\n\n```\nasync function parseCode ( codePath ) { // Load the Python parser const parser = new TreeSitter (); // Set the language to the parser parser . setLanguage ( Python ); // Read the code file content const codeContent = await readFile ( codePath , ' utf8 ' ); // Parse the code using the chosen parser const tree = await parser . parse ( codeContent ); return tree . rootNode ; }\n```\n\n1. **Explore the AST (Doing it on our own! )**\n\n> This can be overwhelming in the beginning, stick with me through this. > \n> \n\nThe AST is a complex data structure, but we can explore its basic elements. Here's a breakdown of the `rootNode` object:\n\n* **rootNode.type:** This property tells you the type of node (e.g., \"function\\_definition\" for our example). * **rootNode.children:** This is an array containing child nodes of the current node. * **rootNode.text:** This property might contain the actual text content of the node (e.g., the function name or the string to be printed).\nHere's some example code to explore the root node of the AST:\n\n```\nasync function printASTNodeInfo ( rootNode ) { console . log ( `Node type:  ${ rootNode . type } ` ); // Loop through child nodes for  ( const child of rootNode . children ) { console . log ( `  - Child node type:  ${ child . type } ` ); // Explore child nodes recursively if  ( child . children . length > 0 ) { await printASTNodeInfo ( child ); } else { // For leaf nodes (no children), print the text content if  ( child . text ) { console . log ( `    - Text content:  ${ child . text } ` ); } } } }\n```\n\n1. **Adding the main function:** Integrating all the functions together\n\n```\nasync function main ( codePath ) { try { const rootNode = await parseCode ( codePath ); await printASTNodeInfo ( rootNode ); } catch  ( error ) { console . error ( `Failed to parse code:  ${ error . message } ` ); }} // Calling the main function main ( YOUR_CODE_PATH );\n```\n\n#### []() Explanation:\n\n1.\n**Recursive Exploration:** The code now includes a nested function call to `printASTNodeInfo` for each child node. This creates a recursive loop, where it explores the child node's type, then further explores its children if it has any. This allows you to traverse the entire AST structure. 2. **Leaf Nodes:** The code checks for the presence of child nodes. If a child node doesn't have any children itself (meaning it's a leaf node), it might contain the actual text content of the code element it represents. The code checks for the `text` property and prints it if available. #### []() Running the Code:\n\n1. Make sure you have Node.js and npm installed. 2. Create two files: `your_code.py` (containing your Python code) and `analyze_code.js` (containing your JavaScript code with the functions above). 3. In `analyze_code.js` , modify the `parseCode` function to return the entire AST ( `tree` ) instead of just the root node. 4.\n ... \n**Example Output:**\n\nAssuming your Python code is the `greet` function example, the output might look something like:\n\n```\nNode type: function_definition\n  - Child node type: identifier\n    - Text content: greet\n  - Child node type: parameter\n    - Text content: name\n  - Child node type: block_statement\n    - Child node type: expression_statement\n      - Child node type: call_expression\n        - Text content: print\n        - Child node type: string_literal\n          - Text content: Hello,  + name + !Node type: module\n  - Child node type: function_definition\nNode type: function_definition\n  - Child node type: def\n    - Text content: def\n  - Child node type: identifier\n    - Text content: greet\n  - Child node type: parameters\nNode type: parameters\n  - Child node type: (\n    - Text content: (\n  - Child node type: identifier\n    - Text content: name\n  - Child node type: )\n    - Text content: )\n....\n```\n\nThis gives you a glimpse into the AST structure, showing the different node types and\npotentially some text content for leaf nodes. Remember, this is a basic exploration, and the AST can get more complex depending on the code you analyze. ### []() Seeing Tree-sitter in Action (Playground Version)\n\nWhile Tree-sitter itself requires some coding knowledge, there are online tools that allow you to see it work! Here's an example using <https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/playground> :\n\n1. Visit the Tree-sitter Playground ( <https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/playground> ). 2. Select \"Python\" from the language dropdown menu. 3. Paste your Python code snippet (like the `greet` function) into the code editor. 4. Click the \"Parse\" button. Yayy! If your code is valid, you'll see the AST generated below the code editor. You can click on different nodes in the tree to see more information about that specific part of the code.\n### []() Beyond the Basics:\n\nWhile exploring the raw AST structure can be informative, some libraries offer functionalities to visualize the AST as a tree structure. These libraries are separate installations and might require additional configuration. However, the core concept remains the same: you use Tree-sitter to generate the AST and then leverage other tools for visualization or further analysis. > [**GitGraph**](https://gitgraph.netlify.app/) is one of my latest project majorly built with the help of tree-sitter. This transforms your codebase (currently supports python only codebases) into an interactive graph, revealing relationships between functions, classes, and files. Drag, zoom, and hover for deep dives, with sidebars displaying neighbor nodes and imported functions. Hyperlinks take you straight to the source code. > Master your Python project's structure with GitGraph's clear and concise dependency view.\nYou can view the backend source code [here](https://github.com/shreshthgoyal/gitgraph-bck) . > Do drop a ⭐ if you like it! > \n> \n\nThis concludes our exploration of Tree-sitter in JavaScript. Remember, this is just the beginning! As you delve deeper into Python development, Tree-sitter can be a valuable tool for understanding complex code structures and making your coding journey more efficient. ### []() Project Ideas\n\nSome project ideas where you can try your hands on:\n\n* Add more language supports to GitGraph. * A browser extension that summarizes code files on GitHub using Tree-sitter, providing quick insights into the code's structure and complexity. * A Code Complexity Analyzer that evaluates the complexity of functions and methods in a codebase, providing recommendations for simplification or refactoring based on the AST.\n ... \n* Location\n  \n  Delhi, India\n* Education\n  \n  IIIT, Gwalior\n* Work\n  \n  Student\n* Joined\n\n### More from [Shreshth Goyal](/shreshthgoyal)\n\n[I Stopped Planning and Just Shipped — Here’s What Happened \\# api \\# indiehacker \\# programming \\# beginners](/shreshthgoyal/i-stopped-planning-and-just-shipped-heres-what-happened-4pch) [Create Your Own AI RAG Chatbot: A Python Guide with LangChain \\# python \\# ai \\# rag \\# langchain](/shreshthgoyal/create-your-own-ai-rag-chatbot-a-python-guide-with-langchain-dfi) [A Guide to Building CLI Tools in JavaScript \\# javascript \\# programming \\# tutorial \\# cli](/shreshthgoyal/a-guide-to-building-cli-tools-in-javascript-28nn)\n\n💎 DEV Diamond Sponsors\n\nThank you to our Diamond Sponsors for supporting the DEV Community\n\n[](https://aistudio.google.com/?utm_source=partner&utm_medium=partner&utm_campaign=FY25-Global-DEVpartnership-sponsorship-AIS&utm_content=-&utm_term=-&bb=146443)\n\nGoogle AI is the official AI Model and Platform Partner of"

## Assistant

All tasks complete. Let me provide a summary of what was done:

### Summary

#### Fixed Issues:

1. **JavaScript highlights.scm compilation error** - The original query file used non-existent node types like `class_expression`. Replaced with the official tree-sitter-javascript queries that use valid node types.

2. **Added missing Msg::Syntax variant and SyntaxMsg enum** - The messages.rs file was missing the `SyntaxMsg` enum and the `Msg::Syntax` variant needed for the Elm architecture to route syntax messages.

3. **Added syntax highlighting worker thread** - The runtime/app.rs was missing:
   - `SyntaxParseRequest` struct
   - `syntax_tx` channel field in `App` struct
   - `syntax_worker_loop` function to process parse requests
   - Command handlers for `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse` and `Cmd::RunSyntaxParse`
   - Initial syntax parsing trigger on startup

#### Added Tests:

1. **Query compilation tests** (`test_all_query_files_compile`) - Verifies all .scm files compile successfully at runtime

2. **Parser tests** - Tests for each language (HTML, CSS, JavaScript, Markdown) plus:
   - `test_incremental_parsing_updates` - Verifies parsing works with updated content
   - `test_revision_tracking` - Verifies revision numbers are preserved
   - `test_document_removal` - Verifies tree cache cleanup

3. **Update flow tests** in syntax.rs:
   - `test_schedule_syntax_parse_for_supported_language`
   - `test_schedule_syntax_parse_skips_plain_text`
   - `test_parse_ready_triggers_run_syntax_parse`
   - `test_parse_ready_skips_stale_revision`
   - `test_parse_completed_stores_highlights`
   - `test_parse_completed_discards_stale_results`
   - `test_full_syntax_update_flow` - End-to-end test of the complete flow

#### Architecture:
The syntax highlighting flow now works as:
1. Edit triggers `redraw_with_syntax_parse()`
2. Returns `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse` with 50ms delay
3. After delay, sends `SyntaxMsg::ParseReady`
4. `update_syntax` returns `Cmd::RunSyntaxParse`
5. Worker thread parses and sends `SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted`
6. Highlights stored in document, `Cmd::Redraw` triggers UI update

## Assistant

Fixed. The issue was that `keymap.yaml` contains multi-byte UTF-8 characters like `→` (3 bytes). Tree-sitter returns byte positions, and when slicing at a byte offset that lands inside a multi-byte character, Rust panics. The fix finds the nearest valid char boundary before slicing.

1) TL;DR
From the code you’ve shown, the async pipeline (revision tracking, stale-result filtering, and clearing highlights in `push_edit`) is logically sound and should not itself cause misaligned highlighting after pressing Enter. The most likely causes are either (a) some `InsertNewline` path that mutates the buffer without going through `push_edit`, or (b) a rendering/UI bug outside these modules that’s drawing old highlight positions against new text.

Below I’ll walk through each of the things you asked to understand and then evaluate your 3 candidate root causes.

---

## 1. How `InsertNewline` interacts with syntax highlighting

Let’s follow the control flow for pressing Enter:

### Multi‑cursor `InsertNewline`

Relevant block (simplified):

```rust
DocumentMsg::InsertNewline => {
    let cursor_before = *model.editor().primary_cursor();

    if model.editor().has_multiple_cursors() {
        let cursors_before: Vec<Cursor> = model.editor().cursors.clone();
        let indices = cursors_in_reverse_order(model);
        let mut operations = Vec::new();

        for idx in indices {
            let cursor = model.editor().cursors[idx];
            let insert_line = cursor.line;
            let pos = model.document().cursor_to_offset(cursor.line, cursor.column);
            model.document_mut().buffer.insert_char(pos, '\n');

            operations.push(EditOperation::Insert {
                position: pos,
                text: "\n".to_string(),
                cursor_before: cursor,
                cursor_after: Cursor::at(cursor.line + 1, 0),
            });

            // Update cursors & selections ...
        }

        let cursors_after: Vec<Cursor> = model.editor().cursors.clone();
        model.document_mut().push_edit(EditOperation::Batch {
            operations,
            cursors_before,
            cursors_after,
        });

        model.document_mut().is_modified = true;
        // ...
        return Some(redraw_with_syntax_parse(model));
    }

    // Single-cursor branch...
}
```

Key points:

- All buffer mutations in this branch happen **before** `push_edit`.
- After applying all newline insertions, you call:

  ```rust
  model.document_mut().push_edit(EditOperation::Batch { ... });
  ```

- `push_edit`:

  ```rust
  pub fn push_edit(&mut self, op: EditOperation) {
      self.undo_stack.push(op);
      self.redo_stack.clear();
      self.is_modified = true;
      self.revision = self.revision.wrapping_add(1);
      // Clear stale syntax highlights
      self.syntax_highlights = None;
  }
  ```

  So every multi‑cursor newline:
  - bumps `revision`
  - sets `syntax_highlights = None` synchronously

- Immediately afterward you return `redraw_with_syntax_parse(model)`.

`redraw_with_syntax_parse`:

```rust
fn redraw_with_syntax_parse(model: &AppModel) -> Cmd {
    if let Some(doc_id) = model.document().id {
        if let Some(parse_cmd) = schedule_syntax_parse(model, doc_id) {
            return Cmd::Batch(vec![Cmd::Redraw, parse_cmd]);
        }
    }
    Cmd::Redraw
}
```

`schedule_syntax_parse`:

```rust
pub fn schedule_syntax_parse(model: &AppModel, document_id: DocumentId) -> Option<Cmd> {
    let doc = model.editor_area.documents.get(&document_id)?;

    if !doc.language.has_highlighting() {
        return None;
    }

    Some(Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse {
        document_id,
        revision: doc.revision, // <-- uses updated revision
        delay_ms: SYNTAX_DEBOUNCE_MS,
    })
}
```

So for multi‑cursor `InsertNewline`:

1. `buffer` is mutated.
2. `push_edit` increments `doc.revision` and clears `syntax_highlights`.
3. `schedule_syntax_parse` reads that **new** `doc.revision`.
4. A `DebouncedSyntaxParse` command is scheduled with that revision.

### Single‑cursor `InsertNewline`

You’ve shown the “selection present” branch:

```rust
if let Some((pos, deleted_text)) = delete_selection(model) {
    model.document_mut().buffer.insert_char(pos, '\n');
    model.set_cursor_from_position(pos + 1);
    model.ensure_cursor_visible();

    let cursor_after = *model.editor().primary_cursor();
    model.document_mut().push_edit(EditOperation::Replace {
        position: pos,
        deleted_text,
        inserted_text: "\n".to_string(),
        cursor_before,
        cursor_after,
    });
}
```

Given the pattern in `InsertChar` and the rest of the file, the “no selection” branch will also:

- modify `buffer`
- update cursor
- call `push_edit(EditOperation::Insert { ... })`
- then return `Some(redraw_with_syntax_parse(model))`

So for the single‑cursor case, the interaction with highlighting is identical: every newline insertion must go through `push_edit`, which clears highlights and increments revision, before scheduling a parse.

**Conclusion:** For the `InsertNewline` message, all visible paths in the code imply:

- Highlights are always cleared immediately after the edit.
- A new parse is always scheduled using the *post‑edit* revision.

---

## 2. Is revision/staleness tracking working?

Let’s walk the full async flow with a concrete scenario (Rust file, press Enter at top):

1. **Initial state (just opened Rust file)**
   - `doc.revision = 0` (or some current value)
   - `doc.syntax_highlights = Some(...)` for that revision.

2. **User presses Enter at start of file**

   - `InsertNewline` mutates `buffer`.
   - `push_edit` runs:
     - `revision` becomes `rev+1` (say `1`)
     - `syntax_highlights = None`
   - `redraw_with_syntax_parse` schedules `DebouncedSyntaxParse { revision: 1, delay_ms: 50 }`.

3. **Debounce fires → `SyntaxMsg::ParseReady { document_id, revision: 1 }`**

   `update_syntax`:

   ```rust
   SyntaxMsg::ParseReady { document_id, revision } => {
       let doc = model.editor_area.documents.get(&document_id)?;

       if doc.revision != revision {
           // stale; bail out
           return None;
       }

       let source   = doc.buffer.to_string();
       let language = doc.language;

       Some(Cmd::RunSyntaxParse {
           document_id,
           revision,
           source,
           language,
       })
   }
   ```

   - It fetches the **current** document.
   - If the user has typed more since pressing Enter:
     - `doc.revision` would be > 1
     - The request `{ revision: 1 }` is now stale → skipped.
   - Only if no new edits happened do we proceed to parsing with the correct snapshot.

4. **Worker thread runs `parse_and_highlight(source, language, doc_id, revision)`**

   - `SyntaxHighlights::new(language, revision)` is called at start.
   - All highlight data is computed freshly from `source`.
   - The resulting `highlights` struct carries that same `revision`.

5. **SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted { document_id, revision: 1, highlights }**

   `update_syntax`:

   ```rust
   SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted { document_id, revision, highlights } => {
       let doc = model.editor_area.documents.get_mut(&document_id)?;

       if doc.revision != revision {
           // stale; discard result
           return None;
       }

       doc.syntax_highlights = Some(highlights);
       Some(Cmd::Redraw)
   }
   ```

   - Again compares `doc.revision` to the `revision` of the parse.
   - If any edits occurred while parsing:
     - `doc.revision` > 1 → discard stale results, do **not** overwrite highlights.
   - Only the parse that exactly matches the current document revision can update `syntax_highlights`.

**So revision/staleness tracking is correct and robust:**

- Both `ParseReady` *and* `ParseCompleted` are guarded.
- A stale `ParseReady` never triggers a parse.
- A stale `ParseCompleted` result is never applied.

This makes a race condition between “old parse” vs “new document” extremely unlikely, unless some edit path forgets to bump `revision`.

---

## 3. Could there be a race between clearing highlights and re-parsing?

From the code:

- Highlight clearing happens synchronously in the same thread when `push_edit` runs.
- Parse scheduling (`DebouncedSyntaxParse`) is created *after* the edit and uses the updated `revision`.
- UI redraw is also driven via `Cmd::Redraw` on that same main/Elm-style loop.

Timeline for a single edit:

1. Edit mutates buffer.
2. `push_edit` sets `syntax_highlights = None` and increments revision.
3. `update_document` returns `Cmd::Batch([Redraw, DebouncedSyntaxParse])`.
4. UI redraw runs: sees `syntax_highlights = None` and draws without any tokens.
5. After debounce+parse, `ParseCompleted` may set new `syntax_highlights` for that specific revision and cause another `Redraw`.

There is no window in which:

- The buffer has been changed,
- Highlights still reflect the old content, **and**
- Those stale highlights are being used – because `push_edit` already set them to `None` before the redraw.

So, provided:

- **All** mutating edit paths go through `push_edit`, and
- No other code sets `syntax_highlights` directly,

then:

> 1. **“Highlights not being cleared during the debounce window”**
>    From the code you’ve shown, this is not happening. The debounce window is a period where `syntax_highlights` is explicitly `None`, not stale.

The one thing to double‑check:

- Search for any direct mutations of `doc.buffer` that don’t culminate in `doc.push_edit(...)` (e.g., special indentation, auto‑formatting, snippets, or file‑load normalization).
  - If any such path runs and still calls `schedule_syntax_parse`, you will get a parse for a changed buffer **without** clearing old highlights → classic misalignment.
  - This is where I’d focus instrumentation: log `(doc_id, revision, syntax_highlights.is_some())` in each edit path.

---

## 4. Are highlight line numbers updated correctly after newlines, especially at the top?

### How line numbers are produced

`parse_and_highlight`:

```rust
let lines: Vec<&str> = source.lines().collect();
...
let node = capture.node;
let start = node.start_position();
let end = node.end_position();
// start.row, end.row are 0-based line numbers from tree-sitter
...
let line = lines.get(row).copied().unwrap_or("");
...
let line_highlights = highlights.lines.entry(row).or_default();
line_highlights.tokens.push(HighlightToken { ... });
```

So:

- Highlight line numbers are **freshly computed** from the current `source` each time.
- They are not “updated” relative to previous highlights; they are recomputed from scratch.
- Keys in `SyntaxHighlights::lines` are 0‑based `row` indices from tree‑sitter.

On the document side:

- `Document::get_line_highlights`:

  ```rust
  pub fn get_line_highlights(&self, line: usize) -> &[HighlightToken] {
      self.syntax_highlights
          .as_ref()
          .and_then(|h| h.get_line(line))
          .map(|lh| lh.tokens.as_slice())
          .unwrap_or(&[])
  }
  ```

  Here it passes the rope line index `line` straight into `SyntaxHighlights::get_line(line)`, so it assumes:

  - rope line index == tree‑sitter row index used when building highlights.

### Is that assumption valid after inserting a newline at the start?

For a Rust file `"fn main() {}\n"`:

- Rope (`buffer`) lines (conceptual):
  - line 0: `"fn main() {}\n"`
  - line 1: `""` (empty final line due to trailing newline)

- `source.lines().collect()` returns:
  - `["fn main() {}"]` (no trailing empty line; Rust’s `str::lines` drops the last empty line)

- Tree‑sitter rows:
  - row 0: `"fn main() {}"`
  - row 1: position after the newline; likely no tokens

Your highlight map:

- `highlights.lines` will have:
  - entry 0 → tokens for `"fn main() {}"`
  - no entry for 1

When you press Enter at the **start**:

- New buffer content: `"\nfn main() {}\n"`
- Rope lines:
  - line 0: first (empty) line
  - line 1: `"fn main() {}\n"`
  - line 2: `""` (empty final line)
- `source.lines().collect()` gives:
  - `["", "fn main() {}"]` (again, no trailing empty)
- Tree‑sitter rows:
  - row 0: empty line
  - row 1: `"fn main() {}"`
  - row 2: position after trailing newline; no tokens

Highlight map is recomputed:

- `highlights.lines[0]` = tokens for first line (empty → probably no tokens)
- `highlights.lines[1]` = tokens for `"fn main() {}"`.

When drawing:

- `Document::get_line_highlights(0)` → no tokens (blank line).
- `Document::get_line_highlights(1)` → tokens for `fn main` etc.

That matches the **new** text layout. No stale line shifting is involved.

The only subtle mismatch here is about the **final empty line** (because `str::lines` skips it while rope counts it), but:

- That affects only the last empty line, not all subsequent lines.
- It will manifest as “no highlighting on the very last empty line”, not as systemic misalignment when you insert a newline at the top.

So with respect to your specific symptom:

> 2. **“Line numbers in highlights becoming stale after text shifts”**
>    From this pipeline, they are not stale: a fresh `SyntaxHighlights` map is built from the full post-edit `source`, keyed by current tree‑sitter rows. That matches the rope line indices for all meaningful lines.

---

## 5. Could this be a revision race?

Given:

- `push_edit` increments `revision` on **every** edit.
- `schedule_syntax_parse` always uses `doc.revision`.
- Both `ParseReady` and `ParseCompleted` discard mismatched revisions.

There is no visible path where an “old” parse result for a pre‑newline buffer could be applied to the **post‑newline** buffer. That is exactly what the revision checks prevent.

> 3. **“Race condition in revision checking”**
>    With the code as written, this is very unlikely. The revision checks are in the right places and use the same `revision` value that was captured at schedule time.

The only way a race could still sneak in is if:

- Some mutating operation changes `doc.buffer` but does **not**:
  - bump `doc.revision`, or
  - clear `syntax_highlights`.

In that case:

- A parse might be scheduled/committed under the old revision, believing the document is unchanged.
- The UI would then happily use `syntax_highlights` that don’t match the buffer.

Again, that points back to: search for *any* code that modifies `buffer` outside of the `update_document`/`push_edit` pattern.

---

## 6. What to check next / how to narrow it down

Given the above, among your three candidate causes, the most plausible is:

- **Some specific path in the Enter-at-start case is bypassing `push_edit` or scheduling a parse for the wrong revision.**

Concrete things to verify (minimal logging):

1. **Every edit path uses `push_edit`**
   - Grep for `buffer.insert_char`, `buffer.remove`, `buffer.insert`, etc.
   - For each, verify that it is followed (logically, not necessarily immediately) by exactly one `push_edit`.
   - Watch out for:
     - Auto‑indent or auto‑brace behavior in the `InsertNewline` branch below the truncated code.
     - Any special-case logic that runs only when `cursor.line == 0 && cursor.column == 0`.

2. **Log the revision flow around the bug**
   - On `push_edit`: `tracing::debug!("push_edit: doc {} -> revision {}", doc_id, self.revision);`
   - In `schedule_syntax_parse`: log `{doc_id, revision}`.
   - In `ParseReady` and `ParseCompleted`: log received `revision`, current `doc.revision`, and whether the command is skipped/applied.
   - Also log `highlights.revision` when stored.

   Then reproduce: open `.rs`, press Enter at start, watch whether:
   - Any `ParseCompleted` with mismatched revision is applied (it shouldn’t be).
   - The revision sequence skips or repeats unexpectedly.

3. **Validate line alignment at draw time**
   - In the rendering code (where you iterate lines and call `get_line_highlights`), log:
     - `line_index`, `line_text_snippet`, number of highlight tokens.
   - After pressing Enter at start, confirm that:
     - The first visible code line’s index matches the index where tokens for `fn` live.

If all of that is consistent, the bug is almost certainly in the **rendering layer** (e.g., mixing screen rows vs. document rows, or caching highlight slices across redraws even when `syntax_highlights` is set to `None`).

---

## 7. When to consider a more advanced design

Only if you later see:

- Performance problems from full reparse on every keystroke, or
- Complex multi‑cursor/structural edit paths that make it hard to guarantee `push_edit` coverage,

would you consider:

- Incremental parsing (using tree‑sitter edits), and/or
- A diff-based highlight update that shifts old line indices instead of recomputing from scratch.

Right now, though, your existing design is simple and, from this code, correct. The bug is likely a missing `push_edit`/revision bump or a UI‑side issue, not a fundamental race or line-number problem in the syntax pipeline.

assert_eq!(model.editor().primary_cursor().column, 0);\n+\n+        // Insert newline at start of file\n+        let cmd = update(&mut model, Msg::Document(DocumentMsg::InsertNewline));\n+\n+        // After the edit:\n+        // 1. ## Assistant

I see - the parser uses `source.lines()` to get lines, and `byte_to_char_col` converts byte columns to character columns. The token columns are character indices into the original line text.

The rendering code uses `document.get_line(doc_line)` which returns the line from the rope, and then does tab expansion. If the rope line contains tabs, the expanded text will have more characters, and the token columns won't match.

But wait - the user said they're opening `samples/sample_code.rs` which (from the od output) doesn't seem to have tabs. Let me look more carefully at the rendering flow.

Actually, I think I found a bug! Look at the parser code line 151:

```rust
let lines: Vec<&str> = source.lines().collect();
```

And then lines are used to compute highlights. But `str::lines()` doesn't include the trailing empty line if the file ends with a newline, and doesn't preserve the newline character.

Meanwhile, in rendering (mod.rs line 537):
```rust
if let Some(line_text) = document.get_line(doc_line) {
```

This gets lines from the Rope, which DOES include trailing newlines.

The critical issue: **the line indices from tree-sitter use 0-based rows matching `source.lines()`, but the rendering uses document line indices from the Rope.**

Wait actually that should be fine - they should both be 0-indexed line numbers.

6. ✅ Revision staleness checks work correctly
7. ✅ Highlight line indices align with rope line indices

### Summary

The model/update/parser layer is thoroughly tested and works correctly. The tests prove that:
- Pressing Enter at start of file correctly clears old highlights
- New highlights are computed with correct line numbers
- The flow from edit → schedule → parse → apply works correctly

If there's still a visual issue, it's likely in:
1. The rendering code (view/mod.rs) - possibly tab expansion misalignment
2. A timing issue in the runtime/app.rs event loop
3. Something specific to the actual files being tested

The comprehensive tests added will help catch regressions and provide confidence that the core syntax highlighting logic is correct.
